\mathbb{R}
”, we conclude (iii) from Theorem
3
, (
S.104
) and Lemma
S.10
.
\Box

S2.5
Proof of Theorem
5

To prove Theorem
5
, we need a preparation. In fact, an asymptotic ratio-consistent estimator of
\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)
will be derived (the notation of “
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
” is given in (
S.109
)). It is stated in Proposition S
1
. We will develop a series of auxiliary results for this purpose.

Review the setting in Section
3.2
. In what follows, we assume the integers
n
,
p
and
q
satisfy
1\leq q<p
and
q<n
.

L
EMMA
S.11

Let
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
be i.i.d. from the
p
-dimensional distribution
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma}).
Write

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{X}_{ia}\\
\mathbb{X}_{ib}\end{pmatrix}\ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ \mathbb{\Sigma}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}&\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ab}\\
\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}&\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb}\end{pmatrix}

for each
i=1,\cdots,n
, where
\mathbb{X}_{ia}
is a
q
-dimensional vector with distribution
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa})
. Then
\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}:=\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb}-\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ab}
is a
(p-q)\times(p-q)
matrix. Recall
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}
in (
16
). We then have

\displaystyle\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\overset{d}{=}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{W}\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}

where
\mathbb{W}
is a
(p-q)\times(n-q)
matrix and the entries are i.i.d.
N(0,1).

Proof of Lemma
S.11
. Recall the notation between (
15
) and (
16
),

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}=({\boldsymbol{X}}_{1a},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{na})^{T},\ \ {\bf H}_{a}={\bf X}_{a}({\bf X}_{a}^{T}{\bf X}_{a})^{-1}{\bf X}_{a}^{T},\ \ {\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}=({\boldsymbol{X}}_{1b},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{nb})^{T},

\displaystyle\tilde{\bf X}_{b}=(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{b},\ \ \hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}=n^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{b}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{b},

where
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}
is
n\times q
,
{\bf H}_{a}
is
n\times n
, both
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}
and
\tilde{\bf X}_{b}
are
n\times(p-q)
and
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}
is
(p-q)\times(p-q)
.
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}
is defined as

\displaystyle\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}=\frac{1}{n}\tilde{\bf X}_{b}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{b}=\frac{1}{n}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}.

(S.108)

Then, by Lemma
S.5
, the
(p-q)
-dimensional random vector
\mathbb{X}_{ib}-\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{ia}\sim N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a})
and is independent of
\mathbb{X}_{ia}
.
It follows that the conditional distribution of
\mathbb{X}_{ib}
given
\mathbb{X}_{ia}
is characterized by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{X}_{ib}|\mathbb{X}_{ia})=N(\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{ia},\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}).

Therefore, we have from the definition
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}=E\mbox{Cov}(\mathbb{X}_{1b}|\mathbb{X}_{1a})
that

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}=\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}.

(S.109)

Write

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}=\big(\mathbb{X}_{1b}-\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{1a},\cdots,\mathbb{X}_{nb}-\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{na}\big)+\big(\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{1a},\cdots,\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{na}\big).

Notice the last two vectors are both normal and they are independent since that
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
are i.i.d. from the
p
-dimensional population
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma})
. Moreover, we can also write

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}=(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})+\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{a}^{T}

(S.110)

where
\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n}
are i.i.d.
(p-q)
-dimensional random vectors with distribution
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a})
also independent of
\mathbb{X}_{a}
. By definition (
S.108
),

\displaystyle\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\big[(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})+\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{a}^{T}\big](\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})\big[(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})+\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{a}^{T}\big]^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})^{T},

(S.111)

since
\mathbb{X}_{a}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})=\mathbb{0}
. Let
\{\xi_{ij};\,1\leq i\leq p-q,1\leq j\leq n\}
be i.i.d.
N(0,1)
-distributed random variables independent of
\mathbb{X}_{a}
. Without loss of generality, assume
\mathbb{V}_{j}=\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\cdot(\xi_{ij})_{(p-q)\times 1}
for each
j
. Therefore,

\displaystyle(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})=\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\cdot(\xi_{ij})_{(p-q)\times n}.

(S.112)

Since the
n\times n
idempotent matrix
{\bf H}_{a}
has rank
q
, we know
\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}
has rank
n-q
. As a function of
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}
,
\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}
is independent of
(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})
. As a result, there exists an
n\times n
random orthogonal matrix
\mathbb{\Gamma}
independent of
\{\xi_{ij};\,1\leq i\leq p-q,1\leq j\leq n\}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}=\mathbb{\Gamma}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{I}_{(n-q)\times(n-q)}&\mathbb{0}\\
\mathbb{0}&\mathbb{0}_{q\times q}\end{pmatrix}\mathbb{\Gamma}^{T},

where the three
\mathbb{0}
above are matrices of entries
0
with proper size. By the orthogonal invariance of i.i.d. standard normals,
(\xi_{ij})_{(p-q)\times n}\mathbb{\Gamma}
has the same distribution as that of
(\xi_{ij})_{(p-q)\times n}
. From (
S2.5
) and (
S.112
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\overset{d}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\cdot(\xi_{ij})_{(p-q)\times n}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{I}_{(n-q)\times(n-q)}&\mathbb{0}\\
\mathbb{0}\mathbb{0}_{q\times q}\end{pmatrix}(\xi_{ij})_{(p-q)\times n}^{T}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{W}\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}

where
\mathbb{W}=(\xi_{ij})_{(p-q)\times(n-q)}
.
\square

L
EMMA
S.12

Recall the notations in Lemma
S.11
and (
S.109
). Let
\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{p-q}
be the eigenvalues of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}=\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}
. Let
\{\mathbb{w}_{i};\,1\leq i\leq p-q\}
be i.i.d.
(n-q)
-dimensional vectors whose entries are i.i.d.
N(0,1).
Then
\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\overset{d}{=}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|^{2}

and

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\overset{d}{=}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}^{2}(\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{i})^{2}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p-q}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}(\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{j})^{2}.

In particular,

\displaystyle E\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)=\frac{n-q}{n^{2}}\cdot\big[\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a})\big]^{2}+\frac{(n-q)(n-q+1)}{n^{2}}\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big).

(S.113)

Proof of Lemma
S.12
. Let
\mathbb{W}
be a
(p-q)\times(n-q)
matrix whose entries are i.i.d.
N(0,1).
Immediately,
E\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\cdot E(\mathbb{W}\mathbb{W}^{T})\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}=\frac{n-q}{n}\cdot\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}
. Using the identity
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{A}\mathbb{B})=\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A})
for any matrices
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
, we know that

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\overset{d}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mbox{tr}\big(\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{W}\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mbox{tr}\big(\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}\mathbb{W}\big)

(S.114)

and

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\overset{d}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mbox{tr}\big[\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{W}\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}\mathbb{W}\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}^{1/2}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mbox{tr}\big[(\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}\mathbb{W})^{2}\big].

(S.115)

Since
\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{p-q}
are the eigenvalues of
\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}
, we are able to decompose
\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}=\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{\Lambda}\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}
, where
\mathbb{\Gamma}_{1}
is an orthogonal matrix and
\mathbb{\Lambda}=\mbox{diag}(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{p-q})
. By the orthogonal invariance of i.i.d.
N(0,1)
-entries, we get

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\overset{d}{=}\frac{1}{n}\mbox{tr}\big[(\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Lambda}\mathbb{W})\big]\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\overset{d}{=}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mbox{tr}\big[(\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Lambda}\mathbb{W})^{2}\big].

Furthermore write
\mathbb{W}^{T}=(\mathbb{w}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{w}_{p-q})
. Then
\mathbb{w}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{w}_{p-q}
are i.i.d.
(n-q)
-dimensional vectors of distribution
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{I}_{n-q})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Lambda}\mathbb{W}=\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}\mathbb{w}_{i}\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T},

which gives

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\overset{d}{=}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|^{2}.

(S.116)

Additionally, we have

\displaystyle(\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Lambda}\mathbb{W})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}^{2}(\mathbb{w}_{i}\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T})^{2}+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p-q}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}(\mathbb{w}_{i}\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T})(\mathbb{w}_{j}\mathbb{w}_{j}^{T}).

Observe

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}[(\mathbb{w}_{i}\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T})^{2}]=\mbox{tr}[(\mathbb{w}_{i}\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T})\mathbb{w}_{i}\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}]=\mbox{tr}[(\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{i}\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{i}]=(\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{i})^{2}.

Similarly,
\mbox{tr}[(\mathbb{w}_{i}\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T})(\mathbb{w}_{j}\mathbb{w}_{j}^{T})]=(\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{j})^{2}
. Then we end up with

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}\big[(\mathbb{W}^{T}\mathbb{\Lambda}\mathbb{W})^{2}\big]=\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}^{2}(\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{i})^{2}+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p-q}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}(\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{j})^{2}.

It follows from (
S2.5
) that

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\overset{d}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}^{2}(\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{i})^{2}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p-q}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}(\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{j})^{2}.

(S.117)

Since
\mathbb{w}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{w}_{p-q}
are i.i.d.
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{I}_{n-q})
, we know
\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{i}\sim\chi^{2}(n-q)
and
\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{j}\overset{d}{=}\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|\cdot\eta
, where
\eta\sim N(0,1)
and is independent of
\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|
. Recall
E\chi^{2}(m)=m
and
\mbox{Var}(\chi^{2}(m))=2m
for any integer
m\geq 1
. Thus, by independence we have
E(\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|^{2}\cdot\eta^{2})=n-q.
From (
S.117
), we obtain

\displaystyle E\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot\big[2(n-q)+(n-q)^{2}\big]\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}^{2}+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot(n-q)\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p-q}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}.

Using the identity

\displaystyle 2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p-q}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}\Big)^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{p-q}\lambda_{i}^{2},

we arrive at

\displaystyle E\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)=\frac{n-q}{n^{2}}\cdot\big[\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a})\big]^{2}+\frac{(n-q)(n-q+1)}{n^{2}}\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)

by using (
S.109
). This completes the proof of the lemma.
\square

L
EMMA
S.13

Let
r\geq 1
and
\mathbb{w}_{1}
and
\mathbb{w}_{2}
be i.i.d.
r
-dimensional random vectors with distribution
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{I}_{r})
. Then the following are true.

(i)
\mbox{Var}((\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{1})^{2})=8r(r+2)(r+3).

(ii)
\mbox{Var}((\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2})=2r(r+3).

(iii)
\mbox{Cov}((\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2},(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{3})^{2})=2r
.

(iv)
\mbox{Cov}((\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{1})^{2},(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2})=4r(r+2).

Proof of Lemma
S.13
. It is well-known that

\displaystyle E[\chi^{2}(r)^{m}]=r(r+2)\cdots(r+2m-2)

(S.118)

for all integers
m\geq 1
. We will use this formula to prove the results.

(i) Since
\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{1}\sim\chi^{2}(r)
, we have

\displaystyle E(\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{4})=E(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{1})^{2}=r(r+2)

(S.119)

and
E(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{1})^{4}=r(r+2)(r+4)(r+6)
. This leads to

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}((\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{1})^{2})=

\displaystyle r(r+2)(r+4)(r+6)-[r(r+2)]^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8r(r+2)(r+3).

(ii) By Proposition 7.3 from
Eaton 1983
or Theorem 1.5.6 from
Muirhead 1982
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \mathbb{e}_{i}:=\frac{\mathbb{w}_{i}}{\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|}\ \ \mbox{are independent}

(S.120)

for
i=1,2
. Therefore,
(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}=\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{2}\cdot(\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}\overset{d}{=}\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{2}\cdot\eta^{2}
, where
\eta\sim N(0,1)
and
\eta
is independent of
\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|
. Consequently, by (
S.119
),

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}((\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2})=

\displaystyle E(\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{4}\cdot\eta^{4})-\big[E(\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{2}\cdot\eta^{2})\big]^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(r+2)\cdot 3-r^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2r(r+3).

(iii) By (
S.120
), we have

\displaystyle(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{3})^{2}=\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{4}\cdot(\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}\cdot(\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{3})^{2}.

Easily, be checking their covariance, we know
\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2}
and
\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{3}
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
. This implies that
\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{4}
,
(\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}
and
(\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{3})^{2}
are independent. Thus, from (
S.119
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}((\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2},(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{3})^{2})=

\displaystyle E\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{4}\cdot 1\cdot 1-E(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}\cdot E(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{3})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(r+2)-r^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2r.

(iv) Write
(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{1})^{2}(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}=\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{6}\cdot(\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}
. From the independence between
\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|
and
\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}((\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{1})^{2},(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{6}\cdot E(\mathbb{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}-E(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{1})^{2}\cdot E(\mathbb{w}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(r+2)(r+4)-r(r+2)\cdot r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4r(r+2).

Thus, the verification is completed.
\square

L
EMMA
S.14

Let
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
be as in (
16
) and (
S.109
), respectively. Then

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\big)\leq\frac{1024(n-q)(n+p-2q)^{2}}{n^{4}}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}).

Proof of Lemma
S.14
. Set
r=n-q
and
s=p-q
. Let
\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{s}
be the eigenvalues of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}=\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}
. Standard computation gives

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}(\gamma_{1}+\cdots+\gamma_{s})=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\mbox{Var}(\gamma_{i})+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq s}\mbox{Cov}(\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j}),

(S.121)

for any random variables
\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{s}
. Then, from Lemma
S.12

we see

\displaystyle n^{4}\cdot\mbox{Var}\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\big)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}^{4}\,\mbox{Var}\big((\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{i})^{2}\big)+4\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq s}\lambda_{i}^{2}\lambda_{j}^{2}\,\mbox{Var}\big((\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{j})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle+8\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq s}\sum_{(k,l)\in A_{i,j}}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{l}\,\mbox{Cov}\big((\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{j})^{2},(\mathbb{w}_{k}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{l})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle+4\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq s}\sum_{k\in\{i,j\}}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}^{2}\,\mbox{Cov}\big((\mathbb{w}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{j})^{2},(\mathbb{w}_{k}^{T}\mathbb{w}_{k})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle C_{1}+C_{2}+C_{3}+C_{4},

(S.122)

where
A_{i,j}=\{(k,l)\neq(i,j);\,1\leq k<l\leq s,\ \{k,l\}\cap\{i,j\}\neq\emptyset\}
. By Lemma
S.13
(i),

\displaystyle C_{1}=8r(r+2)(r+3)\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}^{4}\leq 8(r+3)^{3}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}).

(S.123)

By Lemma
S.13
(ii),

\displaystyle C_{2}=

\displaystyle 4r(r+3)\cdot 2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq s}\lambda_{i}^{2}\lambda_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4(r+3)^{2}\Big(\sum_{1\leq i\leq s}\lambda_{i}^{2}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4(r+3)^{2}\,\big[\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2})\big]^{2}.

(S.124)

By Lemma
S.13
(iii),

\displaystyle C_{3}=

\displaystyle 2r\cdot 8\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq s}\sum_{(k,l)\in A_{i,j}}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{l}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 16r\sum_{1\leq i,j,k\leq s}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 16r\cdot\big[\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a})\big]^{2}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}).

(S.125)

By Lemma
S.13
(iv),

\displaystyle C_{4}\leq

\displaystyle 4r(r+2)\cdot 4\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq s}\sum_{k\in\{i,j\}}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}\lambda_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 16(r+2)^{2}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq s}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 16(r+2)^{2}\big[\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a})\big]\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{3}).

(S.126)

Let
I
be uniformly distributed over
\{1,\cdots,s\}.
By Hölder’s inequality,
(E\lambda_{I}^{\alpha})^{1/\alpha}\leq(E\lambda_{I}^{\beta})^{1/\beta}
for any
0<\alpha<\beta
. This says that

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\lambda_{1}^{\alpha}+\cdots+\lambda_{s}^{\alpha}}{s}\Big)^{1/\alpha}\leq\Big(\frac{\lambda_{1}^{\beta}+\cdots+\lambda_{s}^{\beta}}{s}\Big)^{1/\beta}.

By taking
\alpha=1,2,3
, respectively, and
\beta=4
, we have

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{i})\leq s^{1-(i/4)}\cdot[\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4})]^{i/4}

(S.127)

for
i=1,2,3.
Consequently,

\displaystyle C_{2}\leq 4(r+3)^{2}s\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}),\ \ \ C_{3}\leq 16rs^{2}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}),

\displaystyle C_{4}\leq 16(r+2)^{2}s\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}).

Combing the above with (
S2.5
), we get

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\big)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8(r+3)^{3}+4(r+3)^{2}s+16rs^{2}+16(r+2)^{2}s}{n^{4}}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{16(r+3)(r+s+3)^{2}}{n^{4}}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}).

Using the fact
3\leq 3r
to see
16(r+3)(r+s+3)^{2}\leq 4^{5}r(r+s)^{2}
and plugging in
r=n-q
and
s=p-q
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\big)\leq\frac{4^{5}(n-q)(n+p-2q)^{2}}{n^{4}}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}),

which concludes the proof.
\square

L
EMMA
S.15

Let
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
be as in (
16
) and (
S.109
), respectively. Then, there exists a constant
K>0
not depending on
p,q,n
or
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
such that

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}\Big(\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\Big)\leq\frac{K(n-q)^{2}(p-q)^{2}}{n^{4}}\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}\big).

Proof of Lemma
S.15
. Set
r=n-q
and
s=p-q
. Let
\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{s}
be the eigenvalues of the
s\times s
matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
. Let
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{s}
be i.i.d. random variables with distribution
\chi^{2}(r)
, with
E\xi_{1}=r
and
\mbox{Var}(\xi_{1})=2r
. From (
S.116
),

\displaystyle n^{4}\cdot\mbox{Var}\big(\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\big)=\mbox{Var}\Big(\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}\xi_{i}\Big)^{2}\Big).

(S.128)

Let
U
be a random variable with mean
\mu
. Write
U^{2}=(U-\mu)^{2}+2\mu U-\mu^{2}
. Trivially,
\mbox{Var}(W_{1}+W_{2})\leq 2\,\mbox{Var}(W_{1})+2\,\mbox{Var}(W_{2})
for any random variables
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}(U^{2})\leq

\displaystyle 2\,\mbox{Var}((U-\mu)^{2})+8\mu^{2}\,\mbox{Var}(U)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2E(U-\mu)^{4}+8\mu^{2}\,\mbox{Var}(U).

Now consider
U
to be
U=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}\xi_{i}
, with
\mu=EU=r\,\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a})
. Use (
S.127
) to see

\displaystyle\mu^{2}\cdot\mbox{Var}(U)=2\mu^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}^{2}=2r^{2}\,[\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a})]^{2}\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\leq 2r^{2}s^{2}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}).

(S.129)

By the Marcinkiewtz-Zygmund inequality [see, e.g., Theorem 2 on p. 386 from
Chow and
Teicher 1997
] and the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle E(U-\mu)^{4}=

\displaystyle E\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}(\xi_{i}-r)\Big]^{4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K_{1}\cdot E\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}^{2}(\xi_{i}-r)^{2}\Big]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K_{1}\cdot\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}^{4}\Big)\cdot E\sum_{i=1}^{s}(\xi_{i}-r)^{4},

where
K_{1}
is a numerical constant. Write
\xi_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\eta_{i}
, where
\eta_{1},\cdots,\eta_{r}
are i.i.d.
\chi^{2}(1)
-distributed random variables. By Corollary 2 on p. 387 from
Chow and
Teicher 1997
,
E[\sum_{j=1}^{r}(\eta_{i}-1)]^{4}\leq K_{2}r^{2}
, where
K_{2}
is also a numerical constant. That is,
E(\xi_{1}-r)^{4}\leq K_{2}r^{2}
. In summary, we get
E(U-\mu)^{4}\leq(K_{1}K_{2})r^{2}s\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}\big)
and hence

\displaystyle n^{4}\cdot\mbox{Var}\big(\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\big)=\mbox{Var}(U^{2})\leq(2K_{1}K_{2})r^{2}s\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}\big)+16r^{2}s^{2}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4})

from (
S.128
) and (
S.129
). The conclusion then follows by using the fact
r^{2}s\leq r^{2}s^{2}
.
\square

P
ROPOSITION
1

Let
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
be as in (
16
) and (
S.109
). Set
r=n-q
and

\displaystyle\widehat{\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)}=\frac{n^{2}}{r(r+1)}\cdot\Big[\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{r}\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\Big].

Assume (
20
) holds. If
q=o(n)
,
q=o(p)
and
p=o(n^{3})
then
\widehat{\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)}/\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\to 1
in

probability as
p\to\infty
. Hence,
\widehat{\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)}
is an asymptotic ratio-consistent estimator of
\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)
.

Proof of Proposition
1
. Since
\mbox{Var}(W_{1}+W_{2})\leq 2\,\mbox{Var}(W_{1})+2\,\mbox{Var}(W_{2})
for any random variables
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
, by Lemma
S.14
and Lemma
S.15
, there exists a constant
K>0
independent of
n,p,q
or
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
such that

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}\Big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{r}\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K\cdot\Big[\frac{(n-q)(n+p-2q)^{2}}{n^{4}}+\frac{(n-q)^{2}(p-q)^{2}}{n^{4}r^{2}}\Big]\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K\cdot\Big[\frac{(n+p)^{2}}{n^{3}}+\frac{p^{2}}{n^{4}}\Big]\cdot\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4}).

(S.130)

On the other hand, by (
S.113
),

\displaystyle E\Big[\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{r}\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r}{n^{2}}\cdot\big[\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a})\big]^{2}+\frac{r(r+1)}{n^{2}}\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{r}E\big[\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\big].

(S.131)

Define
s=p-q
. Let
\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{s}
be the eigenvalues of the
s\times s
matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
. By (
S.116
),

\displaystyle n^{2}\cdot E\big[\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\big]=

\displaystyle E\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|^{2}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{s}\lambda_{i}^{2}E\big(\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|^{4})+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq s}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}E\big(\|\mathbb{w}_{i}\|^{2}\|\mathbb{w}_{j}\|^{2}\big),

where
\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{2},\cdots,\|\mathbb{w}_{s}\|^{2}
are i.i.d.
\chi^{2}(r)
-distributed random variables.
From (
S.119
),
E(\|\mathbb{w}_{1}\|^{4})=r(r+2)
. Thus, we have from independence that

\displaystyle n^{2}\cdot E\big[\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\big]=

\displaystyle r(r+2)\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)+r^{2}\cdot 2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq s}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(r+2)\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)+r^{2}\big\{\big[\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}\big)\big]^{2}-\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2r\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)+r^{2}\cdot\big[\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}\big)\big]^{2},

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r}E\big[\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\big]=\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)+\frac{r}{n^{2}}\cdot\big[\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}\big)\big]^{2}.

Combine this with (
S2.5
) to get

\displaystyle EF_{p}=E\Big[\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{r}\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\Big]=\frac{r^{2}+r-2}{n^{2}}\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big),

(S.132)

where
F_{p}=\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{r}\big[\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big]^{2}
. Under Assumption (
20
),

\tau^{-1}<\lambda_{min}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a})\ \leq\lambda_{max}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a})<\tau
for some constant
\tau>1
. This then implies

\displaystyle\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4})=\lambda_{1}^{4}+\cdots+\lambda_{s}^{4}\leq s\tau^{4}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \big[\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\big]^{2}=(\lambda_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{s}^{2})^{2}\geq s^{2}\tau^{-4}.

We deduce from (
S2.5
) and (
S.132
) that

\displaystyle\mbox{Var}\Big(\frac{F_{p}}{EF_{p}}\Big)\leq

\displaystyle K\cdot\frac{n(n+p)^{2}+p^{2}}{(r^{2}+r-2)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{4})}{\big[\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\big]^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(3K)\tau^{8}\cdot\frac{n^{3}+np^{2}}{(r^{2}+r-2)^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{s},

where the fact
n(n+p)^{2}+p^{2}\leq 3(n^{3}+np^{2})
is used in the second inequality.
Under the conditions
q/n\to 0
,
q/p\to 0
and
p=o(n^{3})
, we know
r/n\to 1
and
s/p\to 1
, hence the last term is of order
\frac{1}{np}+\frac{p}{n^{3}}\to 0
. This leads to
\frac{F_{p}}{EF_{p}}\to 1
in probability and we conclude from (
S.132
) that

\displaystyle\Big[\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{r}\big(\mbox{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}\big)\big)^{2}\Big]\cdot\Big[\frac{r(r+1)}{n^{2}}\cdot\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\Big]^{-1}\to 1

in probability. The proof is complete.
\square

L
EMMA
S.16

Let
\mathbb{y}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{y}_{m}
be i.i.d.
p
-dimensional random vectors with distribution
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{B})
, where
\mathbb{B}
is a
p\times p
non-negative definite matrix. Let
\mathbb{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a non-zero random vector independent of
\mathbb{y}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{y}_{m}
. Write
\mathbb{x}_{1}=\mathbb{x}/\|\mathbb{x}\|
and
the
m\times p
matrix
(\mathbb{y}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{y}_{m})^{T}=(\mathbb{z}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{z}_{p})
. Then
(\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{p})^{T}\sim N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{B})
and is independent of
\|\mathbb{x}\|
.

Proof of Lemma
S.16
. Write
\mathbb{B}=(b_{ij})_{p\times p}
and
(\mathbb{y}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{y}_{m})^{T}=(y_{ij})_{m\times p}
. Use the fact that the rows of the matrix are i.i.d. to see
E(y_{ki}y_{lj})=0
if
k\neq l
and
E(y_{ki}y_{lj})=b_{ij}
if
k=l
. Thus,

\displaystyle E(\mathbb{z}_{i}\mathbb{z}_{j}^{T})=\big(E(y_{ki}y_{lj})\big)_{1\leq k,l\leq p}=b_{ij}\mathbb{I}_{p}.

(S.133)

Obviously,
\mathbb{z}_{i}\sim N(0,b_{ii}\mathbb{I}_{m})
for each
i
. Since
\mathbb{x}_{1}
is a unit random vector, then conditional on
\mathbb{x}
, we know
\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{p}
are jointly Gaussian random variables with
\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{i}\sim N(0,b_{ii})
for each
1\leq i\leq p
.
Let us check their covariance matrix. In fact, conditional on
\mathbb{x}
,

\displaystyle E\big[(\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{i})(\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{j})\big]=E\big(\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{i}\mathbb{z}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{x}_{1}\big)=\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}E\big(\mathbb{z}_{i}\mathbb{z}_{j}^{T}\big)\mathbb{x}_{1}=b_{ij},

by (
S.133
) and the fact
\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{x}_{1}=1
. In summary, conditional on
\mathbb{x}
, the random vector
(\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{p})^{T}\sim N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{B})
. Since
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{B})
is free of
\mathbb{x}_{1}
, this implies, unconditionally, it is also true that
(\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{p})^{T}\sim N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{B})
. Finally, for any set
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
G\subset[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle P\big((\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{p})^{T}\in F,\,\|\mathbb{x}\|\in G\big)=

\displaystyle E\big[P\big((\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{p})^{T}\in F\big|\mathbb{x}\big)\cdot I(\|\mathbb{x}\|\in G)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\big[P\big(N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{B})\in F\big)\cdot I(\|\mathbb{x}\|\in G)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P\big(N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{B})\in F\big)\cdot P(\|\mathbb{x}\|\in G).

This shows that
(\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{1}\cdots\mathbb{x}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{z}_{p})^{T}
and
\|\mathbb{x}\|
are independent.
\square

L
EMMA
S.17
(
Bai and
Silverstein 2010
)

Let
\mathbf{A}=\left(a_{ij}\right)
be an
n\times n
non-random matrix and
\mathbf{X}=

\left(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\right)^{T}
be a random vector of independent entries. Assume that
\mathrm{E}x_{i}=0
,
E\left|x_{i}\right|^{2}=1
and
E\left|x_{j}\right|^{\ell}\leq\nu_{\ell}
. Then, for any
p\geq 1
,

E\left|\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{AX}-\operatorname{tr}\mathbf{A}\right|^{p}\leq C_{p}\left(\left(\nu_{4}\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{T}\right)\right)^{p/2}+\nu_{2p}\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{T}\right)^{p/2}\right),

where
C_{p}
is a constant depending on
p
only.

L
EMMA
S.18

Assume
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies that
\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{n}
are i.i.d. with
E\epsilon_{1}=0
,
E\epsilon_{1}^{2}=\sigma^{2}
and
E|\epsilon_{1}|^{2k}<\infty
for an integer
k\geq 2
. Let
\mathbb{H}
be an
n\times n
symmetric, random matrix satisfying
\mathbb{H}^{2}=\mathbb{H}
and
\mbox{rank}\,(\mathbb{H})=q
. Assume
\mathbb{u}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\mathbb{I}_{n})
and that
\mathbb{u},\mathbb{H}
and
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
are independent. Then

(i)
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(n-q)\sigma^{2}+O_{p}(\sqrt{n})
;

(ii)
E(\mathbb{u}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2k}\leq C(k,\sigma)\cdot q^{k}
, where
C(k,\sigma)
is a constant depending on
k,\sigma
only.

Proof of Lemma
S.18
. It is easy to see
E({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\mathbb{A}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=\sigma^{2}\,\mbox{tr}(A)
for any matrix
\mathbb{A}
.

(i) Obviously, the conditional mean
E[{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|{\bf H}]=(n-q)\sigma^{2}.
Take another expectation to see
E[{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})]=(n-q)\sigma^{2}.
By Lemma
S.17
,

\displaystyle E\big[\big({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}-(n-q)\sigma^{2}\big)^{2}\big|{\bf H}\big]\leq

\displaystyle C\cdot E\epsilon_{1}^{4}\cdot\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\cdot E\epsilon_{1}^{4}\cdot(n-q),

where
C>0
is a constant free of
n,q,k,\sigma
. By taking another expectation, we get
\mbox{Var}({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})\leq Cn
. Thus, by the Chebyshev inequality,

\displaystyle P\big(|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}-(n-q)\sigma^{2}|\geq A\sqrt{n}\big)\leq\frac{\mbox{Var}({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})}{A^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{A^{2}}.

This implies
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(n-q)\sigma^{2}+O_{p}(\sqrt{n})
.

(ii) First,
E({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=\sigma^{2}\cdot\mbox{tr}({\bf H})=\sigma^{2}q
. Trivially,

\displaystyle E\big({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{k}\leq

\displaystyle 2^{k}\cdot\big[E\big|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}-\sigma^{2}q\big|^{k}+(\sigma^{2}q)^{k}\big].

From Lemma
S.17
and the fact
{\bf H}^{l}={\bf H}
for any
l=1,2,\cdots
, we see

\displaystyle E\big|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}-\sigma^{2}q\big|^{k}\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}\cdot\big[(E\epsilon_{1}^{4})^{k/2}+E\epsilon_{1}^{2k}\big]\cdot\big[(\mbox{tr}({\bf H}))^{k/2}+\mbox{tr}({\bf H})\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}q^{k/2},

where
C_{k}
is a constant depending on
k
only. Therefore,

\displaystyle E\big({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{k}\leq C(k,\sigma)\cdot q^{k},

(S.134)

where
C(k,\sigma)
is a constant depending on
k,\sigma
only. On the other hand, noting that the
n
entries of
\mathbb{u}
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
, by conditioning on
{\bf H}
and
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, the random variable
\mathbb{u}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
has the distribution of
G\cdot\|{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|
, where
G\sim N(0,1)
. Or, equivalently,

\displaystyle\mbox{given}\ \{{\bf H},{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\},\ \mbox{random variable}\ \mathbb{u}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\ \mbox{has distribution}\ G\cdot\big({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{1/2}.

In particular, this implies that

\displaystyle E(\mathbb{u}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2k}=E\big(G^{2k}\big)\cdot E\big({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{k}.

By combining this, (
S.134
) and the fact
E(G^{2k})=(2k-1)!!
, we obtain

\displaystyle E(\mathbb{u}^{T}{\bf H}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2k}\leq(2k-1!!\cdot C(k,\sigma)\cdot q^{k}.

The proof is completed.
\square

Recall random error vector
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the linear regression model from Section
3.2
. The components
\{\varepsilon_{i};\,1\leq i\leq n\}
are assumed to be i.i.d. random variables.

P
ROPOSITION
2

Let
T^{(3)}_{max}
be defined as in (
18
). Assume (
19
) and (
20
) are true and (
2
) holds with “
\mathbb{\Sigma}
” replaced by “
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
”. Suppose
p=o(n^{3})
,
q=o(p)
,
q\leq n^{\delta}
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
and
E(|\epsilon_{1}|^{\ell})<\infty
with
\ell=14(1-\delta)^{-1}
.
Then, under
H_{0}
from (
14
),
T^{(3)}_{max}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)
converges weakly to a distribution with cdf
F(x)=\exp\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\exp(-\frac{x}{2})\}
.

Proof of Proposition
2
.
Recall the notation between (
15
) and (
16
). In particular,
{\bf H}_{a}={\bf X}_{a}({\bf X}_{a}^{T}{\bf X}_{a})^{-1}{\bf X}_{a}^{T}
. Under the null hypothesis in (
14
),
\mathbb{Y}={\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, where
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the random errors
\{\varepsilon_{i};\,1\leq i\leq n\}
are i.i.d. with
E\varepsilon_{i}=0
and
\mbox{Var}(\varepsilon_{i})=\sigma^{2}
for each
i
. Also,
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is assumed to be independent of
\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i};\,1\leq i\leq n\}
. Use
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})=\mathbb{0}
and
({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}=\mathbb{0}
to see

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})\boldsymbol{Y}=

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})({\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}.

(S.135)

Recalling (
16
), we denote

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}=({\boldsymbol{X}}_{1b},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{nb})^{T}\ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ \tilde{\bf X}_{b}=(\tilde{\bf X}_{q+1},\cdots,\tilde{\bf X}_{p})=(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{b},

(S.136)

where
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}
and
\tilde{\bf X}_{b}
are
n\times(p-q)
matrices. Write
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}=(\mathbb{w}_{q+1},\cdots,\mathbb{w}_{p})
. Then the last assertion from (
S.136
) says that
\tilde{\bf X}_{j}=(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})\mathbb{w}_{j}
for each
q+1\leq j\leq p
. This leads to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{Y}=

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})\mathbb{w}_{j}\mathbb{w}_{j}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})({\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})\mathbb{w}_{j}\mathbb{w}_{j}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

via the facts
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})=\mathbb{0}
and
({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}=\mathbb{0}
again. Note that
(\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j})^{-1}
is a scalar. By the definition of
T^{(3)}_{max}
in (
18
), we derive

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{max}=

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\frac{\boldsymbol{Y}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}(\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j})^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{Y}}{\boldsymbol{Y}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})\boldsymbol{Y}/(n-q)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(n-q)\sigma^{2}}{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}(n^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j})^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big\}.

Set

\displaystyle W_{p}=\frac{(n-q)\sigma^{2}}{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}.

By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\big|T^{(3)}_{max}-W_{p}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big\}\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle W_{p}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\cdot\big|1-(n^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j})^{-1}\big|\big\}.

(S.137)

Define

\displaystyle H_{31}=\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big\};

\displaystyle H_{32}=\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\cdot\big|1-(n^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j})^{-1}\big|\big\}.

By Lemma
S.18
(i), we know
1/W_{p}=1+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
, which implies

\displaystyle W_{p}=1+O_{p}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Big).

(S.138)

We will show that, as
p\to\infty
,

\displaystyle H_{31}^{\prime}:=H_{31}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)\to\mbox{a distribution with cdf}\ e^{-e^{-x/2}/\sqrt{\pi}};\ \ \ \ \ \ \

(S.139)

\displaystyle H_{32}\to 0\ \mbox{in probability}.

(S.140)

Assuming they are true, using assumption
p=o(n^{3})
and (
S2.5
) we have

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{max}=

\displaystyle\Big[1+O_{p}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)\Big]\cdot\big[H_{31}^{\prime}+2\log(p-q)-\log\log(p-q)+o_{p}(1)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{31}^{\prime}+2\log(p-q)-\log\log(p-q)+o_{p}(1).

(S.141)

This and (
S.139
) show that
T^{(3)}_{max}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)
converges weakly to a distribution with cdf
F(x)=\exp\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\exp(-\frac{x}{2})\}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
. It remains to prove (
S.139
) and (
S.140
), which will be done in two steps as follows.

Step 1: the proof of (
S.139
)
. By (
S.110
) and (
S.136
),
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}=(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})+\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{X}_{a}^{T}
,
where
\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n}
are i.i.d.
(p-q)
-dimensional random vectors with distribution
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a})
and they are also independent of
\mathbb{X}_{a}
. In particular,
\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n}
are independent of
{\bf H}_{a}
, a function of
\mathbb{X}_{a}
. As
(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}=\mathbb{0}
, we see that

\displaystyle(\tilde{\bf X}_{q+1},\cdots,\tilde{\bf X}_{p})=

\displaystyle(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})\big[\mathbb{X}_{a}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{aa}^{-1}\mathbb{\Sigma}_{ba}^{T}+(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})^{T}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})^{T}.

Write
(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})^{T}=(\mathbb{u}_{q+1},\cdots,\mathbb{u}_{p})
. In other words, each
\mathbb{u}_{q+1},\cdots,\mathbb{u}_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a column of
(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})^{T}
. Immediately we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\bf X}_{j}=(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})\mathbb{u}_{j}\ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ \mathbb{u}_{j}\sim N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{I}_{n})

(S.142)

for each
j=q+1,\cdots,p.
This means
\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}-\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, which then leads to

\displaystyle(\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}=

\displaystyle\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{2}+(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}-2({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j})\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

(S.143)

by using the fact
\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}={\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}.
Obviously, it holds that

\displaystyle\big|\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}(\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}\big\}-\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{2}\big\}\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}\big\}+2\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\big|({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j})\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big|\big\}.

(S.144)

A key observation is that the three random quantities
\mathbb{u}_{j}
,
{\bf H}_{a}
and
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
are independent.
By the last assertion from (
S.142
) and Lemma
S.18
(ii), we have

\displaystyle E(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2k}\leq C(k,\sigma)\cdot q^{k}

(S.145)

for any
q+1\leq j\leq n
, where
k=[\frac{6}{1-\delta}]+1
and
C(k,\sigma)
is a constant depending on
k,\sigma
only.
We next show

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}\to 0\ \ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ \ \frac{1}{n}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big|({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j})\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big|\to 0

(S.146)

in probability as
p\to\infty
. In fact, for any
\beta>0
,

\displaystyle P\Big(\frac{1}{n}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}\geq\beta\Big)\leq

\displaystyle p\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}P\Big((\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}\geq n\beta\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\frac{E(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2k}}{(n\beta)^{k}}.

Therefore, from assumption
q\leq n^{\delta}
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle P\Big(\frac{1}{n}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}\geq\beta\Big)\leq C(\beta,k,\sigma)\cdot\frac{pq^{k}}{n^{k}}\leq C(\beta,k,\sigma)\cdot\frac{p}{n^{k(1-\delta)}},

where
C(\beta,k,\sigma)
is a constant depending on
\beta,k
and
\sigma
. Since
k(1-\delta)>6
, we get the first limit of (
S.146
) by using the assumption
p=o(n^{3})
. For the second limit, by setting
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}={\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}/\|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big|({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j})\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big|=\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big|({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j})\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big|.

By the law of large numbers,
\|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|/\sqrt{n}\to\sigma
in probability. Thus, to get the second limit of (
S.146
), it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big|({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j})\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big|\to 0

(S.147)

in probability. Similar to an earlier argument, we have from the fact
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j}\sim N(0,1)
that

\displaystyle P\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big|({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j})\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big|\geq\beta\Big)\leq

\displaystyle p\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}P\Big(|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j}|\cdot|\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|\geq\sqrt{n}\beta\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{p}{(\sqrt{n}\beta)^{k}}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}E\big[|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j}|^{k}\cdot|\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}|^{k}\big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(k,\beta)\cdot\frac{p}{n^{k/2}}\cdot\big[E(\mathbb{u}_{1}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2k}\big]^{1/2}

by the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and
E(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2k}=E(\mathbb{u}_{1}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2k}
, where
C(k,\beta)
is a constant depending on
k
and
\beta
only. Use (
S.145
) to see

\displaystyle P\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big|({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j})\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\bf H}_{a}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big|\geq\beta\Big)\leq C(k,\beta,\sigma)\cdot\frac{pq^{k/2}}{n^{k/2}}=O\Big(\frac{p}{n^{k(1-\delta)/2}}\Big),

by the assumption
q\leq n^{\delta}
again. Since
p=o(n^{3})
and
k(1-\delta)>6
, we get (
S.147
) and then the second limit of (
S.146
).

Now we study
\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{2}\big\}
in (
S2.5
). Since
\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{n}
are i.i.d. with mean zero and variance
\sigma^{2}
. By assumption,
E(|\epsilon_{1}|^{\ell})<\infty
with
\ell=14(1-\delta)^{-1}
. This concludes
E(\epsilon_{1}^{14})<\infty
. Write
\|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}=\epsilon_{1}^{2}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n}^{2}
. By the central limit theorem,
n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}=1+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
. Use
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}={\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}/\|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|
to see

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{2}\big\}=

\displaystyle\big(n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}\|{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}\big)\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big[1+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})\big]\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1}\big)^{2}.

(S.148)

Review
\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n}
are i.i.d.
(p-q)
-dimensional random vectors with distribution
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a})
and
(\mathbb{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{V}_{n})^{T}=(\mathbb{u}_{q+1},\cdots,\mathbb{u}_{p})
.
By Lemma
S.16
,

\displaystyle(\mathbb{u}_{q+1}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{u}_{p}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1})^{T}\sim N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}).

(S.149)

Based on assumption, (
2
) holds with “
\mathbb{\Sigma}
” replaced by “
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
”, we know Assumption (
2
) also holds by the discussion below (
2
). We then have from Theorem
2
that

\displaystyle P\big(\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1})^{2}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)\leq x\big)\to\exp\Big\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x/2}\Big\}

as
p\to\infty
. Denote
U_{p}=\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{1})^{2}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)
. Then the above says
U_{p}
converges weakly to the Gumbel distribution with cdf
F(x)=\exp\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x/2}\}
. It then follows from (
S2.5
) that

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{2}\big\}=

\displaystyle\big[1+O_{p}(n^{-1/2})\big]\cdot\big[U_{p}+2\log(p-q)-\log\log(p-q)\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U_{p}+2\log(p-q)-\log\log(p-q)+O_{p}(n^{-1/2}\log p).

Obviously, the last term goes to zero since
p=o(n^{3})
by assumption. Use the Slutsky lemma to see that

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)^{2}\big\}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)

converges weakly to the Gumbel distribution with cdf
F(x)=\exp\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x/2}\}
.
We then obtain (
S.139
) by (
S2.5
) and (
S.146
).

Step 2: the proof of (
S.140
)
. Easily, by definition,

\displaystyle|H_{32}|\leq|H_{31}|\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big|1-(n^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j})^{-1}\big|.

By (
S.139
),
H_{31}=O(\log p)
. Thus, to prove (
S.140
), it suffices to
show

\displaystyle H_{32}^{\prime}:=\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big|1-(n^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j})^{-1}\big|=o_{p}\Big(\frac{1}{\log p}\Big).

(S.150)

Now we prove this assertion. From assumption
q\leq n^{\delta}
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, we choose
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,\min\{1/2,1-\delta\}).
This indicates that

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\frac{1}{2},\delta\Big\}<1-\delta^{\prime}<1.

(S.151)

Note that, for
x>0
and
s\in(0,1)
satisfying
|1-x^{-1}|\geq s
, one of the two inequalities
x^{-1}\geq 1+s
and
x^{-1}\leq 1-s
must hold. Equivalently,
x\leq\frac{1}{1+s}
or
x\geq\frac{1}{1-s}
. Both of them imply that
|x-1|\geq\frac{s}{1+s}\geq\frac{1}{2}s
. Consequently,

\displaystyle P\Big(H_{32}^{\prime}\geq n^{-\delta^{\prime}}\Big)\leq

\displaystyle p\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}P\Big(\big|1-(n^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j})^{-1}\big|\geq n^{-\delta^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}P\Big(\Big|\frac{1}{n}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}-1\Big|\geq\frac{1}{2}n^{-\delta^{\prime}}\Big).

Recalling (
S.142
) and that the matrix
(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})^{2}=\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}
has rank
n-q
, we know
\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}\sim\chi^{2}(n-q).
Denote
n^{\prime}=n-q
. Observe that
|\frac{y}{n}-1|\geq(1/2)n^{-\delta^{\prime}}
implies
|y-n|\geq(1/2)n^{1-\delta^{\prime}}
, which implies
|y-n^{\prime}|\geq(1/2)n^{1-\delta^{\prime}}-q
. Use (
S.151
) and the assumption
q\leq n^{\delta}
to see
|y-n^{\prime}|/\sqrt{n^{\prime}}\geq[(1/2)n^{1-\delta^{\prime}}-q]/\sqrt{n^{\prime}}\sim(1/2)n^{(1/2)-\delta^{\prime}}.
By definition,
0<(1/2)-\delta^{\prime}<1/2
. Similar to (
S.99
), we have

\displaystyle P\big(H_{32}^{\prime}\geq n^{-\delta^{\prime}}\big)\leq

\displaystyle p\cdot P\Big(\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n^{\prime}}}(\chi^{2}(n^{\prime})-n^{\prime})\Big|\geq\frac{1}{3}n^{(1/2)-\delta^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p\cdot\exp\big(C\cdot n^{1-2\delta^{\prime}}\big),

which is equal to
o(1)
by assumption
p=o(n^{3})
. This says
H_{32}^{\prime}=o_{p}(n^{-\delta^{\prime}})
, which implies (
S.150
), and hence (
S.140
) holds as aforementioned.
\square

Recall the error vector
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the linear regression from Section
3.2
. We assume
\{\varepsilon_{i};\,1\leq i\leq n\}
are i.i.d. random variables.

P
ROPOSITION
3

Let
T^{(3)}_{sum}
be defined as in (
17
). Assume (
19
) and (
20
) hold and (
2
) also holds with “
\mathbb{\Sigma}
” replaced by “
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
”. Suppose
p=o(n^{3})
,
q=o(p)
,
q\leq n^{\delta}
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
and
E(|\epsilon_{1}|^{\ell})<\infty
with
\ell=14(1-\delta)^{-1}
.
Under
H_{0}
from (
14
) we have
T^{(3)}_{sum}
converges to
N(0,1)
in distribution as
p\to\infty
.

Proof of Proposition
3
. Recall the notation from Section
3.2
. In particular,

\displaystyle\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}=n^{-1}\tilde{\bf X}_{b}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{b},\ \ \ \ \hat{\sigma}^{2}=(n-q)^{-1}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{b|a})}=\frac{n^{2}}{(n+1-q)(n-q)}\Big\{\mathrm{tr}\big(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{n-q}\mathrm{tr}^{2}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{b|a})\Big\}

and

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{sum}=\frac{n^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}-n^{-1}(n-q)(p-q)\hat{\sigma}^{2}}{\sqrt{2\hat{\sigma}^{4}\,\widehat{\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)}}}.

(S.152)

Next we will first derive a workable form for the main ingredient
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}
above.

Recall
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{Y}}
. Under the null hypothesis in (
14
),
\mathbb{Y}={\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{a}+{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, where
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\{\varepsilon_{i};\,1\leq i\leq n\}
are i.i.d. random variables with
E\varepsilon_{1}=0
and
\mbox{Var}(\varepsilon_{1})=\sigma^{2}
. By assumption,
\{\varepsilon_{i};\,1\leq i\leq n\}
are also independent of
\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i};\,1\leq i\leq n\}
. Recalling (
S.136
), we denote

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}=({\boldsymbol{X}}_{1b},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{nb})^{T}\ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ \tilde{\bf X}_{b}=(\tilde{\bf X}_{q+1},\cdots,\tilde{\bf X}_{p})=(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}.

Since
{\bf H}_{a}={\bf X}_{a}({\bf X}_{a}^{T}{\bf X}_{a})^{-1}{\bf X}_{a}^{T}
, both
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})=\mathbb{0}
and
({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}=\mathbb{0}
. Thus,
\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
. By definition,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{1}{n-q}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\frac{1}{n-q}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

(S.153)

and

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|(\tilde{\bf X}_{q+1},\cdots,\tilde{\bf X}_{p})^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=q+1}^{p}(\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}.

It is easy to verify that
E({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=\sigma^{2}\mbox{tr}({\bf I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})=(n-q)\sigma^{2}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle E\hat{\sigma}^{2}=\sigma^{2}.

(S.154)

According to (
S.142
),
\tilde{\bf X}_{j}=(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a})\mathbb{u}_{j}
and
\mathbb{u}_{j}\sim N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{I}_{n})
. Hence,

\displaystyle(\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})^{2}=[\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}]^{2}=\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}\cdot(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{2},

(S.155)

where
\mathbb{e}_{2}:=(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}/\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|
. Eventually, we arrive at an ideal form to work with, that is,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\frac{1}{n}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\cdot\sum_{j=q+1}^{p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{2}.

(S.156)

Now we start to prove the central limit theorem.
The assumption
E(|\epsilon_{1}|^{\ell})<\infty
with
\ell=14(1-\delta)^{-1}
implies that
E(|\epsilon_{1}|^{2k})<\infty
with
k=[\frac{6}{1-\delta}]+1
. It follows from Lemma
S.18
(i) that

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(n-q)\sigma^{2}+O_{p}(\sqrt{n}).

(S.157)

This and (
S.153
) imply that

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{2}=\sigma^{2}+O_{p}(n^{-1/2}).

(S.158)

By assumption
q\leq n^{\delta}
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, we see

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\sigma^{2}+O_{p}\big(n^{-\delta^{\prime}}\big),

(S.159)

with
\delta^{\prime}=\min\{1-\delta,1/2\}
.

In lieu of the explanation before (
S.142
),
\{\mathbb{u}_{j};\,q+1\leq j\leq p\}
are independent of
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{a}
and
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
, and hence are independent of the unit random vector
\mathbb{e}_{2}
. We then have from Lemma
S.16
that
(\mathbb{u}_{q+1}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2},\cdots,\mathbb{u}_{p}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{T}
has distribution
N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a})
and is also independent of
\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|
. By assumption, (
2
) holds with “
\mathbb{\Sigma}
” replaced by “
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
”. From Theorem
1
and (
S.109
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=q+1}^{p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{2}-(p-q)}{\sqrt{2\,\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2})}}\to N(0,1)

(S.160)

in distribution as
p\to\infty.
From (
S.153
), we know
\frac{1}{n}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\frac{n-q}{n}\hat{\sigma}^{2}
. By (
S.156
) and (
S.160
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{n\hat{\sigma}^{-2}}{n-q}\cdot\big(n^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\big)-(p-q)}{\sqrt{2\,\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2})}}\to N(0,1).

Use the fact
n/(n-q)\to 1
to see

\displaystyle\frac{n^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}-n^{-1}(n-q)(p-q)\hat{\sigma}^{2}}{\sqrt{2\hat{\sigma}^{4}\,\mbox{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2})}}\to N(0,1).

(S.161)

By Proposition
1
,
\widehat{\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)}/\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\to 1
in probability. We then arrive at

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{sum}=\frac{n^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}-n^{-1}(n-q)(p-q)\hat{\sigma}^{2}}{\sqrt{2\hat{\sigma}^{4}\,\widehat{\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)}}}\to N(0,1)

through an application of the Slutsky lemma. The proof is completed.
\square

Proof of Theorem
5
.
Parts (i) and (ii) follow from Propositions S
3
and S
2
, respectively. The setting here is the same as those in the two propositions, so we will continue to use the same notation in the two propositions to prove (iii) for the asymptotic independence. Recall the definition

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{sum}=\frac{n^{-1}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}-n^{-1}(n-q)(p-q)\hat{\sigma}^{2}}{\sqrt{2\hat{\sigma}^{4}\,\widehat{\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)}}}.

By (
S.153
) and (
S.156
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{b}^{T}\hat{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}=\frac{n-q}{n}\hat{\sigma}^{2}\sum_{j=q+1}^{p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{2}.

Thus,

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{sum}=

\displaystyle\frac{n-q}{n}\cdot\frac{\sum_{j=q+1}^{p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{2}-(p-q)}{\sqrt{2\widehat{\mathrm{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{b|a})}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{T}^{(3)}_{sum}+(\omega_{p}-1)\cdot\tilde{T}^{(3)}_{sum}-\frac{q}{n}\cdot\omega_{p}\tilde{T}^{(3)}_{sum}

where

\displaystyle\tilde{T}^{(3)}_{sum}=:\frac{\sum_{j=q+1}^{p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{2}-(p-q)}{\sqrt{2\mathrm{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{b|a})}}\ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ \omega_{p}:=\Big(\frac{\mathrm{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{b|a})}{\widehat{\mathrm{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{b|a})}}\Big)^{1/2}.

By Proposition
1
,
\widehat{\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)}/\mbox{tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}^{2}\big)\to 1
in probability. Hence
\omega_{p}\to 1
in probability. Also,
\tilde{T}^{(3)}_{sum}\to N(0,1)
by (
S.160
). This together with the assumption
q=O(n^{\delta})
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
implies that

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{sum}=\frac{\sum_{j=q+1}^{p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{2}-(p-q)}{\sqrt{2\,\mathrm{tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}_{b|a})}}+o_{p}(1).

(S.162)

On the other hand, based on (
S.139
) and (
S2.5
),

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{max}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{31}^{\prime}+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big\{n^{-1}\sigma^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}\tilde{\bf X}_{j}^{T}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big\}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\Big\{\frac{\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}}{n\sigma^{2}}\cdot\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\big)^{2}\Big\}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)+o_{p}(1)

(S.163)

by(
S.155
).
Remember that
\mathbb{e}_{2}=(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}/\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|
is independent of
\mathbb{u}_{j}
because
\mathbb{u}_{j},\mathbb{H}_{a},{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
are independent. It follows that

\displaystyle\Big|\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\Big\{\frac{\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}}{n\sigma^{2}}\cdot\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\big)^{2}\Big\}-\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\big)^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}}{n\sigma^{2}}-1\Big|\cdot\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\big)^{2}.

(S.164)

According to (
S.159
), we know that

\displaystyle\frac{\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}}{n\sigma^{2}}=\frac{1}{n\sigma^{2}}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{T}(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=1+O_{p}\big(n^{-\delta^{\prime}}\big)

with
\delta^{\prime}=\min\{1-\delta,1/2\}
.
As a consequence,

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}}{n\sigma^{2}}-1\Big|=O_{p}\Big(\frac{1}{n^{\delta^{\prime}}}\Big).

(S.165)

From the discussion between (
S.159
) and (
S.160
), it holds that

\displaystyle(\mathbb{u}_{q+1}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2},\cdots,\mathbb{u}_{p}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{T}\sim N(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{\Sigma}_{bb\cdot a}).

(S.166)

By assumption, (
2
) holds with “
\mathbb{\Sigma}
” replaced by “
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{b|a}
”, we know Assumption (
2
) also holds by the discussion below (
2
). Due to Theorem
2
, we get

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{2}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)\ \mbox{converges weakly to a distribution}

\displaystyle\mbox{with cdf}\ \exp\Big\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x/2}\Big\}

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
as
p\to\infty
. By assumption,
p=o(n^{3})
, the above particularly implies
\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\mathbb{e}_{2})^{2}=O(\log n)
. This together with (
S2.5
) and (
S.165
) concludes that

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\Big\{\frac{\|(\mathbb{I}_{n}-{\bf H}_{a}){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|^{2}}{n\sigma^{2}}\cdot\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\big)^{2}\Big\}=\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\big)^{2}+o_{p}(1).

According to (
S2.5
), we have

\displaystyle T^{(3)}_{max}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{q+1\leq j\leq p}\big(\mathbb{u}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{e}_{2}\big)^{2}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)+o_{p}(1).

Joining this with (
S.162
) and (
S.166
), by Theorem
3
and Lemma
S.10
, we obtain
T^{(3)}_{sum}
and
T^{(3)}_{max}-2\log(p-q)+\log\log(p-q)
are asymptotically independent.
\square
